Bounded analysis

Q-S1 · CONDITIONAL_RECONSTRUCTION

Kasliwal J. separately reasoned that the expired licence, fire-related vacating and absence of force or unlawful dispossession defeated Section 6 restoration in these circumstances. This is his opinion only, not the Bench's unified holding; the contrary opinion and referral remain unresolved in this document.

Q-S2 · CONDITIONAL_RECONSTRUCTION

Ramaswamy J. separately relied on the concession, retained intention to possess and due-process protection of settled possession to uphold Section 6 restoration on his analysis. This is not a unified Bench merits decision or resolution of the regular lease/licence and title controversy.

Decisive counterarguments and gaps retained verbatim
{
  "counterarguments": [
    "1. Procedural concession versus independent examination of the fire: Ramaswamy J. relies upon the company's express concession that the bank was dispossessed as alleged for the purposes of the suit and regards subsequent denial as impermissible (IK-1144022:L166-L168). Kasliwal J. instead examines the fire, relocation and bank's correspondence to conclude that possession had already been lost without force or unlawful conduct by the company (IK-1144022:L96-L106). The concession's precise procedural consequences remain central to the disagreement.",
    "2. Voluntary vacation versus conditional delivery: The company contended that the bank voluntarily vacated following vis major and that a later refusal to redeliver possession was not statutory dispossession (IK-1144022:L145-L147). The contrary analysis emphasizes that the bank handed over access to facilitate repairs, retained belongings and maintained an intention to resume possession (IK-1144022:L137-L139, IK-1144022:L168). The distinction between loss of physical occupation and loss of legally relevant possession is decisive.",
    "3. Force versus due course of law: Kasliwal J. finds the absence of force and other unlawful means sufficient in the particular circumstances to defeat the Section 6 suit (IK-1144022:L105-L109). Ramaswamy J. interprets the section as protecting against possession obtained through any method outside due course of law, including nonviolent, deceptive or ostensibly innocuous conduct (IK-1144022:L174-L189). Consequently, absence of physical violence does not dispose of the claim under Ramaswamy J.'s approach.",
    "4. Licence expiry versus settled-possession protection: Kasliwal J. treats the expired licence and trespassory occupation as reasons why the bank cannot recover possession from the owner (IK-1144022:L90-L112). Ramaswamy J. acknowledges that post-expiry occupation may be trespassory but maintains that long, settled possession cannot be disturbed without due process (IK-1144022:L201-L210, IK-1144022:L240-L243). The essential opposition concerns the legal consequences of trespassory status, not merely its descriptive classification.",
    "5. Ownership and admitted licence versus the summary remedy's independence from title: Kasliwal J. considers the admitted ownership and the bank's admitted licence status material to denying restoration (IK-1144022:L87-L90, IK-1144022:L110-L111). Ramaswamy J. emphasizes that Section 6 focuses on dispossession, consent, due process and timeliness, while leaving competing title claims to regular litigation (IK-1144022:L153-L164, IK-1144022:L175-L177). The High Court similarly reserved substantive rights while granting provisional restoration (IK-1144022:L83-L85).",
    "6. Precedential distinction between tenants and licensees: Kasliwal J. differentiates cases protecting tenants holding over from cases involving expired licensees and relies upon the trespasser language of K.K. Verma and Lallu Yeshwant Singh (IK-1144022:L117-L124). Ramaswamy J. considers the precise question of an expired licensee under Section 6 unresolved by those tenant cases and draws upon authorities protecting established possession against unilateral recovery (IK-1144022:L223-L226, IK-1144022:L229-L240). The cited precedents must remain attributed to the opinions interpreting them.",
    "7. Scope of D.H. Maniar: Kasliwal J. relies on its conclusion that a person remaining after termination of a licence has no continuing status as a licensee (IK-1144022:L125). Ramaswamy J. answers that D.H. Maniar concerned tenant-status protection under Section 15A of a particular rent-control statute and did not determine the availability of Section 6 restoration (IK-1144022:L203-L206). Its force as a controlling analogy is contested.",
    "8. Settled possession versus transient trespass: Ramaswamy J. invokes authorities distinguishing effective, open and sustained possession from stray, intermittent or concealed trespass and treats more than fifteen years of occupation as settled possession (IK-1144022:L214-L221, IK-1144022:L240-L241). Kasliwal J. stresses expiry of the licence, refusal to renew, absence of acquiescence and subsequent loss of possession following the fire (IK-1144022:L90-L107). Neither approach should be reduced to an unconditional rule governing every trespasser.",
    "9. Restoration despite eventual lack of title: Ramaswamy J. treats immediate protection against self-help as distinct from a later suit establishing ownership and entitlement to possession (IK-1144022:L175-L177, IK-1144022:L199-L200, IK-1144022:L240-L244). Kasliwal J. emphasizes that the company was the true owner and that separate title-based proceedings were available or already initiated (IK-1144022:L110-L116). These positions reflect competing conceptions of the purpose and practical operation of summary restoration.",
    "10. Public-institution conduct versus adherence to the statutory remedy: Kasliwal J. regards the bank's prolonged occupation, public character and contribution to unnecessary litigation as important grounds for denying discretionary relief (IK-1144022:L112-L116). Ramaswamy J. acknowledges litigation abuse and delay but reasons that they cannot justify removal of a person in settled possession outside the prescribed process (IK-1144022:L131, IK-1144022:L242-L244). The High Court's criticism of the bank was expressly prima facie and not an adjudication of substantive rights (IK-1144022:L80-L85).",
    "11. Renewal and classification remain distinct from summary restoration: The bank sought renewal and the company refused it (IK-1144022:L60-L61). Kasliwal J. treats the licence classification and expiration as admitted (IK-1144022:L89-L90). Ramaswamy J. notes the contractual renewal covenant and reserves the underlying lease-or-licence controversy for the regular civil suit (IK-1144022:L209-L210). Neither the bank's claimed renewal nor the ultimate substantive classification should be silently determined by the Section 6 reference.",
    "12. Statutory appeal bar versus constitutional scrutiny: The bank argued that Section 6's bar on appeal and review should prevent Supreme Court interference under Article 136 (IK-1144022:L150). Ramaswamy J. expressly rejects that jurisdictional argument, holding Article 136 review plenary (IK-1144022:L252). This issue must be separated from his conclusion that the bank was entitled to restoration.",
    "13. Opposing proposed dispositions and the actual Bench order: Kasliwal J. would allow the appeal and dismiss the Section 6 suit (IK-1144022:L126). Ramaswamy J. would dismiss the appeal and sustain the High Court decree (IK-1144022:L251-L253). The concluding order instead calls for placement before the Chief Justice of India to constitute a larger bench (IK-1144022:L257). The procedural referral identifies the unresolved disagreement; it supplies no substantive majority merits holding."
  ],
  "gaps": [
    "The available document ends in a larger-bench referral, not a unified merits decision; no follow-up is obtained in this batch.",

    "Readable reference retained with its original contract status: FORMAT_ERROR. No response was repaired into success.",
    "Reference counterargument 5 says provisional restoration; source review corrects this to the Section 6 decree after treating the motion as the trial. Original reference remains unchanged."

The Syndicate Bank executed a leave and licence agreement in favour of the company in respect of 15,000 sq. ft.\non the mezzanine to the ground floor of the hotel Oberoi Towers situated at Nariman Point, Bombay on a monthly compensation of Rs. 60,0007- per month, belonging to the company, for a period of 12 years. The company also stated that as they were cramped for space, the bank should vacate the premises at the end of the term. The company by its letter dated 9th August, 1986 informed the bank that the request for renewal of licence was not acceptable and again requested the bank to handover vacant possession on the expiry of the term. The bank did not handover vacant possession of the premises even after December, 1986 and sent the monthly compensation to the bank.\nThe company did not accept the amount nor acquiesced in the continuance of possession of the bank after the expiry of the period of licence which came to an end on 31st December, 1986. Some correspondence went on between the parties but the company did not agree for the extension of the period of licence. This is his opinion only, not the Bench's unified holding; the contrary opinion and referral remain unresolved in this document.

R2@2 · OPEN_TEXT

Ramaswamy J. separately relied on the concession, retained intention to possess and due-process protection of settled possession to uphold Section 6 restoration on his analysis. This is not a unified Bench merits decision or resolution of the regular lease/licence and title controversy.

    "description": "Ramaswamy J. separately relied on the concession, retained intention to possess and due-process protection of settled possession to uphold Section 6 restoration on his analysis. This is not a unified Bench merits decision or resolution of the regular lease/licence and title controversy.",

    "conclusion_text": "Ramaswamy J. separately relied on the concession, retained intention to possess and due-process protection of settled possession to uphold Section 6 restoration on his analysis. This is not a unified Bench merits decision or resolution of the regular lease/licence and title controversy.",

    "stage": "Supreme Court two conflicting opinions and larger-bench referral",
    "origin": "RESEARCH_TRANSLATION",
    "source_refs": [

    "source_quote": "24. A reading thereof manifests the legislative intent that if any person is dispossessed from immovable property without his consent otherwise than in due course of law, the person dispossessed or any person claiming through him, is empowered to lay the suit to recover possession thereof, notwithstanding any other title that may be set up in such a suit. But it shall be laid before expiry of six months from the date of dispossession. Such suit shall not lie against the Government.\nThe decree or any order passed in that suit shall not be subject to any appeal or review. But it is no bar to any person to sue to establish his title to such property and to recover possession thereof under cite18†Section 5 , in the manner provided in cite19†the CPC , 1908. The question, therefore, is whether the respondent has been dispossessed without his consent.\nIt is clear that the respondent had handed over a part of the premises, to the appellant, due to vis major occurred on April 12, 1990 in the hotel and pursuant to a request made by the appellant that too to comply with the conditions laid by Fire Brigade. It was asserted by the respondent that the appellant had promised to re-deliver possession. It also claimed that it had a right to renewal under the contract. Since civil suit is pending, it is not necessary to consider whether it is a lease or licence.\nSuffice to proceed as pleaded that it is a licence. Sri Nariman conceded in the High Court as extracted hereinbefore, that without prejudice to their right and for the purpose of the suit, the respondent was dispossessed in the manner spoken to in the plaint. The High Court also proceeded on this footing. The counsel for the respondent therein, though objected to and insisted upon the trial of the suit, he agreed to argue, due to the concession made by the counsel for the appellant.\nIn fairness to Shri Nariman, he reiterated before us that he made the concession as extracted by the High Court. In my considered view, it is not open to the appellant to contend now that the respondent had not been dispossessed from the suit premises.\nAs stated in the pleadings and is clear from the documents filed in support thereof that the respondent claimed that it delivered possession to the appellants only to effect renovation and refurnishing of the suit premises with an undertaking to redeliver possession. They retained animus possedendie. It is clear from the fact that part of the furniture is still in part of the demised premises. It must be concluded that they have been dispossessed without their consent.\n27. The contention of Sri Nariman that dispossession by any means, fair or foul unless it is visited with breach of peace and is not amenable to exercise of power under cite11†Section 6 , would be fraught with insidious and pernicious effects on the efficacy of the rule of law as it germinates, generates or inculcates disbelief and apathy therein and drives the people to extra legal remedies.\nIn my considered view, therefore, that possession obtained either by violence, misrepresentation, fraud, coercion, undue influence, seemingly innocuous but hidden with oblique motive or any other mode or method unlawful or otherwise except in due course of law attracts cite11†Section 6 . Law frowns upon such conduct. The court accords legitimacy and legality only to possession taken in due course of law.\n45. Law respects possession even if there is no title to support it. No-one is permitted to take law in one's own hands and to dispossess the person in actual settled possession without due course of law. No person can be allowed to become a judge in his own case. The object of cite11†Section 6 is to discourage people to act in self help, however, good their title may be.\nNo doubt long delay in disposal of cases due to docket explosion became a ruse to unscrupulous litigant to abuse the due course of law to protract litigation and remain in unjust or wrongful possession of the property. Landlord could be suitably compensated by award of damages. It cannot, by any stretch of imagination, be said that a person in settled possession, though unlawful, is not entitled to the protection under cite11†Section 6 of the Act.\n48. In cite22†Puran Singh Sahni 's case the question therein was the distinction between licence and lease vis-a-vis the power of the Tribunal under cite41†Section 91 of the Co-op. Societies Act. It is also of no avail to the appellant. Thus I hold that the respondent, after expiry of the licence or its termination, was in settled possession, and its dispossession was otherwise than in due course of law. Therefore, the decree for possession under cite11†Section 6 of the Act is not illegal.",

By an agreement dated 27th December, 1974 the amount of Rs. 30 lacs was advanced to the company on interest at the rate of 12.5 per cent per annum and the amount was repayable in ten years. The Syndicate Bank executed a leave and licence agreement in favour of the company in respect of 15,000 sq. ft.\non the mezzanine to the ground floor of the hotel Oberoi Towers situated at Nariman Point, Bombay on a monthly compensation of Rs. 60,0007- per month, belonging to the company, for a period of 12 years. The company by a letter dated 17th September, 1984 reminded the bank that the agreement was going to expire on 31st December, 1986. The company also stated that as they were cramped for space, the bank should vacate the premises at the end of the term. This is not a unified Bench merits decision or resolution of the regular lease/licence and title controversy.",
        "jurisdiction": "India; target judgment historical scope only",
        "stage": "Supreme Court two conflicting opinions and larger-bench referral",
        "origin": "RESEARCH_TRANSLATION",
        "source_refs": [
          "IK-1144022:L163",
          "IK-1144022:L164",
          "IK-1144022:L165",
          "IK-1144022:L166",
          "IK-1144022:L167",

        "source_quote": "24. A reading thereof manifests the legislative intent that if any person is dispossessed from immovable property without his consent otherwise than in due course of law, the person dispossessed or any person claiming through him, is empowered to lay the suit to recover possession thereof, notwithstanding any other title that may be set up in such a suit. But it shall be laid before expiry of six months from the date of dispossession. Such suit shall not lie against the Government.\nThe decree or any order passed in that suit shall not be subject to any appeal or review. But it is no bar to any person to sue to establish his title to such property and to recover possession thereof under cite18†Section 5 , in the manner provided in cite19†the CPC , 1908. The question, therefore, is whether the respondent has been dispossessed without his consent.\nIt is clear that the respondent had handed over a part of the premises, to the appellant, due to vis major occurred on April 12, 1990 in the hotel and pursuant to a request made by the appellant that too to comply with the conditions laid by Fire Brigade. It was asserted by the respondent that the appellant had promised to re-deliver possession. It also claimed that it had a right to renewal under the contract. Since civil suit is pending, it is not necessary to consider whether it is a lease or licence.\nSuffice to proceed as pleaded that it is a licence. Sri Nariman conceded in the High Court as extracted hereinbefore, that without prejudice to their right and for the purpose of the suit, the respondent was dispossessed in the manner spoken to in the plaint. The High Court also proceeded on this footing. The counsel for the respondent therein, though objected to and insisted upon the trial of the suit, he agreed to argue, due to the concession made by the counsel for the appellant.\nIn fairness to Shri Nariman, he reiterated before us that he made the concession as extracted by the High Court. In my considered view, it is not open to the appellant to contend now that the respondent had not been dispossessed from the suit premises.\nAs stated in the pleadings and is clear from the documents filed in support thereof that the respondent claimed that it delivered possession to the appellants only to effect renovation and refurnishing of the suit premises with an undertaking to redeliver possession. They retained animus possedendie. It is clear from the fact that part of the furniture is still in part of the demised premises. It must be concluded that they have been dispossessed without their consent.\n27. The contention of Sri Nariman that dispossession by any means, fair or foul unless it is visited with breach of peace and is not amenable to exercise of power under cite11†Section 6 , would be fraught with insidious and pernicious effects on the efficacy of the rule of law as it germinates, generates or inculcates disbelief and apathy therein and drives the people to extra legal remedies.\nIn my considered view, therefore, that possession obtained either by violence, misrepresentation, fraud, coercion, undue influence, seemingly innocuous but hidden with oblique motive or any other mode or method unlawful or otherwise except in due course of law attracts cite11†Section 6 . Law frowns upon such conduct. The court accords legitimacy and legality only to possession taken in due course of law.\n45. Law respects possession even if there is no title to support it. No-one is permitted to take law in one's own hands and to dispossess the person in actual settled possession without due course of law. No person can be allowed to become a judge in his own case. The object of cite11†Section 6 is to discourage people to act in self help, however, good their title may be.\nNo doubt long delay in disposal of cases due to docket explosion became a ruse to unscrupulous litigant to abuse the due course of law to protract litigation and remain in unjust or wrongful possession of the property. Landlord could be suitably compensated by award of damages. It cannot, by any stretch of imagination, be said that a person in settled possession, though unlawful, is not entitled to the protection under cite11†Section 6 of the Act.\n48. In cite22†Puran Singh Sahni 's case the question therein was the distinction between licence and lease vis-a-vis the power of the Tribunal under cite41†Section 91 of the Co-op. Societies Act. It is also of no avail to the appellant. Thus I hold that the respondent, after expiry of the licence or its termination, was in settled possession, and its dispossession was otherwise than in due course of law. JUDGMENT

IK-1144022:L45 · JUDGMENT_TEXT

IK-1144022:L46 · JUDGMENT_TEXT
